========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… they sent me here because of what happened at the station. I’m an EMT, and I was in the bay, and the airway bags were wrong. Not wrong like someone stocked them wrong, but the way they were sitting there, the order of them, it was off, and I knew it had to be put back into the right pattern.

So I kept moving them around, lining them up the way it needed to be, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it. I know that probably sounds odd, but it felt like if I stopped in the middle, or if I missed one, the whole bay would go bad somehow. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I would say it felt excessive exactly. I mean, standing here now, talking about it, I can hear how it sounds—me moving the airway bags around and saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, and not stopping when my lieutenant told me to step away. I get that from the outside it probably looked like I was just… stuck on it.

But in the moment it didn’t feel optional. It felt like the bags had to be in the right pattern, like there was a correct way they were supposed to sit, and if I stopped before it was finished or left one out of place, then the whole bay would go bad somehow. Not like I had a neat explanation for it, but it felt very clear to me right then. Like breaking the sequence would mess everything up.

So part of me knew my lieutenant was getting upset and that I was supposed to listen, but another part of me was just focused on getting it right. I wasn’t trying to be difficult. I just couldn’t step away until it felt right.

I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I was aware in the sense that I knew I was in the bay, I knew my lieutenant was there, I could hear him talking to me, and I knew I was touching the airway bags. It wasn’t like I woke up somewhere else or anything like that.

But it also wasn’t normal awareness, if that makes sense. I was so locked onto the bags and the numbers that everything else felt kind of… pushed back. Like the bay was still there, but it was all less important than getting the order right. I remember pieces of it pretty clearly—the feel of the handles, the numbers I was saying, him telling me to stop—but I don’t know if I could tell you exactly how long it went on. Time was not really what I was paying attention to.

I don’t think I “went somewhere else” in my mind, exactly. I didn’t black out. I just got narrowed down into that one thing, and it felt hard to come back out of it until the pattern was finished, or until people made me stop.

I'm Dr. Smith. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… they sent me here because of what happened at the station. I’m an EMT, and I was in the bay, and the airway bags were wrong. Not wrong like someone stocked them wrong, but the way they were sitting there, the order of them, it was off, and I knew it had to be put back into the right pattern.\n\nSo I kept moving them around, lining them up the way it needed to be, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it. I know that probably sounds odd, but it felt like if I stopped in the middle, or if I missed one, the whole bay would go bad somehow. My lieutenant kept telling me to step away and stop touching the bags, but I couldn’t just break the sequence like that. That’s why they told me I had to come in.\n\nDoctor: When you were lining up the bags and repeating the numbers, did part of you feel that this need to keep things in order was excessive or didn't make sense, even though you still felt driven to do it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I would say it felt excessive exactly. I mean, standing here now, talking about it, I can hear how it sounds—me moving the airway bags around and saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, and not stopping when my lieutenant told me to step away. I get that from the outside it probably looked like I was just… stuck on it.\n\nBut in the moment it didn’t feel optional. It felt like the bags had to be in the right pattern, like there was a correct way they were supposed to sit, and if I stopped before it was finished or left one out of place, then the whole bay would go bad somehow. Not like I had a neat explanation for it, but it felt very clear to me right then. Like breaking the sequence would mess everything up.\n\nSo part of me knew my lieutenant was getting upset and that I was supposed to listen, but another part of me was just focused on getting it right. I wasn’t trying to be difficult. I just couldn’t step away until it felt right.\n\nDoctor: During the time you were arranging the bags and repeating the numbers, did you feel fully aware and present, or did it feel like you lost track of time, went somewhere else in your mind, or had trouble remembering parts of it afterward?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I was aware in the sense that I knew I was in the bay, I knew my lieutenant was there, I could hear him talking to me, and I knew I was touching the airway bags. It wasn’t like I woke up somewhere else or anything like that.\n\nBut it also wasn’t normal awareness, if that makes sense. I was so locked onto the bags and the numbers that everything else felt kind of… pushed back. Like the bay was still there, but it was all less important than getting the order right. I remember pieces of it pretty clearly—the feel of the handles, the numbers I was saying, him telling me to stop—but I don’t know if I could tell you exactly how long it went on. Time was not really what I was paying attention to.\n\nI don’t think I “went somewhere else” in my mind, exactly. I didn’t black out. I just got narrowed down into that one thing, and it felt hard to come back out of it until the pattern was finished, or until people made me stop.\n\n[Last Question]\nNow, looking back on it, do you still think that something bad would really have happened to the bay if you hadn't gotten the bags in the right order, or do you see now that this idea wasn't actually true?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / emergency_medical_technician / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I know the injury review people and some supervisors have been watching me through the station cameras and changing my triage notes to make me look unsafe, even when they tell me it's just routine paperwork.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it’s as simple as saying, “Oh, I see now it wasn’t true.” I know that’s probably the answer people want me to give, like, yes, I was stressed and the bags didn’t matter and nothing would’ve happened. And maybe with the bags specifically, I can sit here and say maybe the bay wouldn’t have… I don’t know, exploded or failed or whatever. I know that sounds unreasonable when you put it that way.

But the reason it felt so important is because things at the station haven’t just been normal. It’s not just me being picky about equipment. I know the injury review people have been watching me through the station cameras, and some of the supervisors too. They say it’s routine, they say the cameras are just there for safety or property or whatever, but it’s not just that. They’re watching when I restock, when I write up calls, when I’m in the bay, and then somehow my triage notes get changed around later so it makes me look unsafe, like I’m missing things or making bad decisions.

So when you ask if I “still think something bad would happen,” I mean… yes, in a way. Not like the bay has feelings, but if things are out of order, it gives them something. It gives them a way to say I’m careless, or unstable, or unsafe with equipment. That’s what I was trying to prevent. I was trying to get everything lined up correctly so they couldn’t twist it afterward. They always make it sound like paperwork, like review, but I know what I’ve written and I know when it’s been made to look different.

I was so locked onto the bags and the numbers that everything else felt kind of… pushed back. Like the bay was still there, but it was all less important than getting the order right. I remember pieces of it pretty clearly—the feel of the handles, the numbers I was saying, him telling me to stop—but I don’t know if I could tell you exactly how long it went on. Time was not really what I was paying attention to.\n\nI don’t think I “went somewhere else” in my mind, exactly. I didn’t black out. I just got narrowed down into that one thing, and it felt hard to come back out of it until the pattern was finished, or until people made me stop.\n\nDoctor: Now, looking back on it, do you still think that something bad would really have happened to the bay if you hadn't gotten the bags in the right order, or do you see now that this idea wasn't actually true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s as simple as saying, “Oh, I see now it wasn’t true.” I know that’s probably the answer people want me to give, like, yes, I was stressed and the bags didn’t matter and nothing would’ve happened. And maybe with the bags specifically, I can sit here and say maybe the bay wouldn’t have… I don’t know, exploded or failed or whatever. I know that sounds unreasonable when you put it that way.\n\nBut the reason it felt so important is because things at the station haven’t just been normal. It’s not just me being picky about equipment. I know the injury review people have been watching me through the station cameras, and some of the supervisors too. They say it’s routine, they say the cameras are just there for safety or property or whatever, but it’s not just that. They’re watching when I restock, when I write up calls, when I’m in the bay, and then somehow my triage notes get changed around later so it makes me look unsafe, like I’m missing things or making bad decisions.\n\nSo when you ask if I “still think something bad would happen,” I mean… yes, in a way. Not like the bay has feelings, but if things are out of order, it gives them something. It gives them a way to say I’m careless, or unstable, or unsafe with equipment. That’s what I was trying to prevent. I was trying to get everything lined up correctly so they couldn’t twist it afterward. They always make it sound like paperwork, like review, but I know what I’ve written and I know when it’s been made to look different.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from believing you're being watched and your notes altered, have you ever heard voices, sounds, or seen things that others couldn't hear or see?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / emergency_medical_technician / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so—not in the way you mean, I don’t think.

Like, I’m not sitting there hearing a voice talking to me when nobody’s around, or seeing a person standing there that other people say isn’t there. I mean, at the station there are always noises—radios, doors, equipment, people moving around—so sometimes I’ll hear something and turn my head, but that’s normal in that place. It’s noisy. It’s not like I can say, “I heard a voice no one else could hear.”

And seeing things… no, I don’t think I’ve seen things that weren’t there. I’m careful saying it because I know how this sounds once people start asking these questions, and I don’t want everything I say to get put in the wrong box. But no, I haven’t noticed anything like that. My issue isn’t that I’m seeing ghosts or hearing voices.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n5\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F20.9, F42\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome in. I'm Dr. Smith. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… they sent me here because of what happened at the station. I’m an EMT, and I was in the bay, and the airway bags were wrong. Not wrong like someone stocked them wrong, but the way they were sitting there, the order of them, it was off, and I knew it had to be put back into the right pattern.\n\nSo I kept moving them around, lining them up the way it needed to be, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it. I know that probably sounds odd, but it felt like if I stopped in the middle, or if I missed one, the whole bay would go bad somehow. My lieutenant kept telling me to step away and stop touching the bags, but I couldn’t just break the sequence like that. That’s why they told me I had to come in.\n\nDoctor: When you were lining up the bags and repeating the numbers, did part of you feel that this need to keep things in order was excessive or didn't make sense, even though you still felt driven to do it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I would say it felt excessive exactly. I mean, standing here now, talking about it, I can hear how it sounds—me moving the airway bags around and saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, and not stopping when my lieutenant told me to step away. I get that from the outside it probably looked like I was just… stuck on it.\n\nBut in the moment it didn’t feel optional. It felt like the bags had to be in the right pattern, like there was a correct way they were supposed to sit, and if I stopped before it was finished or left one out of place, then the whole bay would go bad somehow. Not like I had a neat explanation for it, but it felt very clear to me right then. Like breaking the sequence would mess everything up.\n\nSo part of me knew my lieutenant was getting upset and that I was supposed to listen, but another part of me was just focused on getting it right. I wasn’t trying to be difficult. I just couldn’t step away until it felt right.\n\nDoctor: During the time you were arranging the bags and repeating the numbers, did you feel fully aware and present, or did it feel like you lost track of time, went somewhere else in your mind, or had trouble remembering parts of it afterward?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I was aware in the sense that I knew I was in the bay, I knew my lieutenant was there, I could hear him talking to me, and I knew I was touching the airway bags. It wasn’t like I woke up somewhere else or anything like that.\n\nBut it also wasn’t normal awareness, if that makes sense. I was so locked onto the bags and the numbers that everything else felt kind of… pushed back. Like the bay was still there, but it was all less important than getting the order right. Time was not really what I was paying attention to.\n\nI don’t think I “went somewhere else” in my mind, exactly. I didn’t black out. I know that sounds unreasonable when you put it that way.\n\nBut the reason it felt so important is because things at the station haven’t just been normal. It’s not just me being picky about equipment. I know the injury review people have been watching me through the station cameras, and some of the supervisors too. They say it’s routine, they say the cameras are just there for safety or property or whatever, but it’s not just that. They’re watching when I restock, when I write up calls, when I’m in the bay, and then somehow my triage notes get changed around later so it makes me look unsafe, like I’m missing things or making bad decisions.\n\nSo when you ask if I “still think something bad would happen,” I mean… yes, in a way. Not like the bay has feelings, but if things are out of order, it gives them something. It gives them a way to say I’m careless, or unstable, or unsafe with equipment. That’s what I was trying to prevent. I was trying to get everything lined up correctly so they couldn’t twist it afterward. They always make it sound like paperwork, like review, but I know what I’ve written and I know when it’s been made to look different.\n\nDoctor: Aside from believing you're being watched and your notes altered, have you ever heard voices, sounds, or seen things that others couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nLike, I’m not sitting there hearing a voice talking to me when nobody’s around, or seeing a person standing there that other people say isn’t there. I just got narrowed down into that one thing, and it felt hard to come back out of it until the pattern was finished, or until people made me stop.\n\nDoctor: Now, looking back on it, do you still think that something bad would really have happened to the bay if you hadn't gotten the bags in the right order, or do you see now that this idea wasn't actually true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s as simple as saying, “Oh, I see now it wasn’t true.” I know that’s probably the answer people want me to give, like, yes, I was stressed and the bags didn’t matter and nothing would’ve happened. And maybe with the bags specifically, I can sit here and say maybe the bay wouldn’t have… I don’t know, exploded or failed or whatever. I know that sounds unreasonable when you put it that way.\n\nBut the reason it felt so important is because things at the station haven’t just been normal. It’s not just me being picky about equipment. I know the injury review people have been watching me through the station cameras, and some of the supervisors too. They say it’s routine, they say the cameras are just there for safety or property or whatever, but it’s not just that. They’re watching when I restock, when I write up calls, when I’m in the bay, and then somehow my triage notes get changed around later so it makes me look unsafe, like I’m missing things or making bad decisions.\n\nSo when you ask if I “still think something bad would happen,” I mean… yes, in a way. Not like the bay has feelings, but if things are out of order, it gives them something. It gives them a way to say I’m careless, or unstable, or unsafe with equipment. That’s what I was trying to prevent. I was trying to get everything lined up correctly so they couldn’t twist it afterward. They always make it sound like paperwork, like review, but I know what I’ve written and I know when it’s been made to look different.\n\nDoctor: Aside from believing you're being watched and your notes altered, have you ever heard voices, sounds, or seen things that others couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nLike, I’m not sitting there hearing a voice talking to me when nobody’s around, or seeing a person standing there that other people say isn’t there. I mean, at the station there are always noises—radios, doors, equipment, people moving around—so sometimes I’ll hear something and turn my head, but that’s normal in that place. It’s noisy. It’s not like I can say, “I heard a voice no one else could hear.”\n\nAnd seeing things… no, I don’t think I’ve seen things that weren’t there. I’m careful saying it because I know how this sounds once people start asking these questions, and I don’t want everything I say to get put in the wrong box. But no, I haven’t noticed anything like that. My issue isn’t that I’m seeing ghosts or hearing voices.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you believed that you're being watched and that your notes are being altered—has this been going on for weeks, or more like six months or longer?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / emergency_medical_technician / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\nmanifestation: \"The camera and paperwork stuff, and the station behavior that got everyone alarmed, have been going on for about the last two months.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not six months. I mean, not like this.

The camera and paperwork stuff—me noticing it and feeling like it was directed at me, and then the things I was doing in the station that got everyone upset—that’s been more like the last couple of months. About two months, I guess, if I’m trying to put a number on it.

It’s hard because some of the cameras have always been there, and paperwork review has always existed, so it’s not like one day cameras suddenly appeared. But the part where I started feeling sure they were watching me specifically, and that my notes weren’t staying the way I wrote them, that’s more recent. Weeks, yes, but not just one bad week. More like two months of it building and then getting worse.

I'm Dr. Smith. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… they sent me here because of what happened at the station. I’m an EMT, and I was in the bay, and the airway bags were wrong. Not wrong like someone stocked them wrong, but the way they were sitting there, the order of them, it was off, and I knew it had to be put back into the right pattern.\n\nSo I kept moving them around, lining them up the way it needed to be, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it. I know that probably sounds odd, but it felt like if I stopped in the middle, or if I missed one, the whole bay would go bad somehow. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess if I’m being honest, there were changes before the camera and paperwork thing got really clear to me.

It’s not like I woke up one day and everything was different, but looking back, I was doing less. A lot less. Most days when I wasn’t working, I’d just sit in my apartment, and it would be like the whole day went by and I hadn’t really done anything. I’ve got these trees—or plants, whatever—in the corner, and they’d just sit there needing attention, and I’d notice it, but I couldn’t make myself get up and deal with them. Same with food. I’d buy food and it would just stay in the fridge until it went bad, because cooking felt like this whole complicated project even though I used to cook all the time.

And I stopped calling the guys to play basketball. That used to be a normal thing for me. I didn’t make some big decision like, “I’m quitting basketball,” I just… didn’t call. Didn’t answer sometimes. Didn’t feel like explaining. It wasn’t even that I was enjoying being alone. I’d just sit there and think I should get up, I should do something, and then I wouldn’t.

So, yeah, maybe three to six months ago people probably could’ve noticed I wasn’t myself. I didn’t really want to call it anything at the time. I just kept thinking I was tired, or burned out, or I’d catch up later. But I wasn’t doing the regular stuff I used to do, and I couldn’t really get myself to care enough to start.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d call it “sad” exactly. Not most of the day, every day, like crying or thinking everything was hopeless. It wasn’t that kind of feeling, at least not how I understand it.

It was more like… I just couldn’t get myself to start anything. Like I’d be sitting in my apartment and the whole day would kind of disappear. The trees in the corner would need water or trimming or whatever, and I’d see them there, I knew they needed something, but I’d just keep sitting. Same with food in the fridge—I’d buy stuff thinking I’d cook like I used to, and then it would just sit there until I had to throw it out. Cooking felt like too much, even though it’s not like I don’t know how to do it.

And basketball, too. I used to call the guys, make plans, show up. Then I just stopped. Not because I was sitting there feeling miserable and saying, “What’s the point of life?” It was more like the idea of calling, getting dressed, going out, talking to people—there was no push in me to do it. I couldn’t make myself care enough to start.

So, yeah, more motivation and energy, I guess. I wasn’t myself, but I don’t think it was exactly being depressed.

That’s why they told me I had to come in.\n\nDoctor: When you were lining up the bags and repeating the numbers, did part of you feel that this need to keep things in order was excessive or didn't make sense, even though you still felt driven to do it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I would say it felt excessive exactly. I mean, standing here now, talking about it, I can hear how it sounds—me moving the airway bags around and saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, and not stopping when my lieutenant told me to step away. I get that from the outside it probably looked like I was just… stuck on it.\n\nBut in the moment it didn’t feel optional. It felt like the bags had to be in the right pattern, like there was a correct way they were supposed to sit, and if I stopped before it was finished or left one out of place, then the whole bay would go bad somehow. Not like I had a neat explanation for it, but it felt very clear to me right then. Like breaking the sequence would mess everything up.\n\nSo part of me knew my lieutenant was getting upset and that I was supposed to listen, but another part of me was just focused on getting it right. I wasn’t trying to be difficult. I just couldn’t step away until it felt right.\n\nDoctor: During the time you were arranging the bags and repeating the numbers, did you feel fully aware and present, or did it feel like you lost track of time, went somewhere else in your mind, or had trouble remembering parts of it afterward?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I was aware in the sense that I knew I was in the bay, I knew my lieutenant was there, I could hear him talking to me, and I knew I was touching the airway bags. It wasn’t like I woke up somewhere else or anything like that.\n\nBut it also wasn’t normal awareness, if that makes sense. I was so locked onto the bags and the numbers that everything else felt kind of… pushed back. Like the bay was still there, but it was all less important than getting the order right. I remember pieces of it pretty clearly—the feel of the handles, the numbers I was saying, him telling me to stop—but I don’t know if I could tell you exactly how long it went on. Time was not really what I was paying attention to.\n\nI don’t think I “went somewhere else” in my mind, exactly. I didn’t black out. I just got narrowed down into that one thing, and it felt hard to come back out of it until the pattern was finished, or until people made me stop.\n\nDoctor: Now, looking back on it, do you still think that something bad would really have happened to the bay if you hadn't gotten the bags in the right order, or do you see now that this idea wasn't actually true?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s as simple as saying, “Oh, I see now it wasn’t true.” I know that’s probably the answer people want me to give, like, yes, I was stressed and the bags didn’t matter and nothing would’ve happened. And maybe with the bags specifically, I can sit here and say maybe the bay wouldn’t have… I don’t know, exploded or failed or whatever. I know that sounds unreasonable when you put it that way.\n\nBut the reason it felt so important is because things at the station haven’t just been normal. It’s not just me being picky about equipment. I know the injury review people have been watching me through the station cameras, and some of the supervisors too. They say it’s routine, they say the cameras are just there for safety or property or whatever, but it’s not just that. They’re watching when I restock, when I write up calls, when I’m in the bay, and then somehow my triage notes get changed around later so it makes me look unsafe, like I’m missing things or making bad decisions.\n\nSo when you ask if I “still think something bad would happen,” I mean… yes, in a way. Not like the bay has feelings, but if things are out of order, it gives them something. It gives them a way to say I’m careless, or unstable, or unsafe with equipment. That’s what I was trying to prevent. I was trying to get everything lined up correctly so they couldn’t twist it afterward. They always make it sound like paperwork, like review, but I know what I’ve written and I know when it’s been made to look different.\n\nDoctor: Aside from believing you're being watched and your notes altered, have you ever heard voices, sounds, or seen things that others couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nLike, I’m not sitting there hearing a voice talking to me when nobody’s around, or seeing a person standing there that other people say isn’t there. I mean, at the station there are always noises—radios, doors, equipment, people moving around—so sometimes I’ll hear something and turn my head, but that’s normal in that place. It’s noisy. It’s not like I can say, “I heard a voice no one else could hear.”\n\nAnd seeing things… no, I don’t think I’ve seen things that weren’t there. I’m careful saying it because I know how this sounds once people start asking these questions, and I don’t want everything I say to get put in the wrong box. But no, I haven’t noticed anything like that. My issue isn’t that I’m seeing ghosts or hearing voices.\n\nDoctor: How long have you believed that you're being watched and that your notes are being altered—has this been going on for weeks, or more like six months or longer?\n\nPatient: Not six months. I mean, not like this.\n\nThe camera and paperwork stuff—me noticing it and feeling like it was directed at me, and then the things I was doing in the station that got everyone upset—that’s been more like the last couple of months. About two months, I guess, if I’m trying to put a number on it.\n\nIt’s hard because some of the cameras have always been there, and paperwork review has always existed, so it’s not like one day cameras suddenly appeared. But the part where I started feeling sure they were watching me specifically, and that my notes weren’t staying the way I wrote them, that’s more recent. Weeks, yes, but not just one bad week. More like two months of it building and then getting worse.\n\nDoctor: Before these last two months, going back further—say three to six months ago—did you or people close to you notice any changes in how you were functioning, your motivation, or your usual routines, even before the camera and paperwork concerns started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess if I’m being honest, there were changes before the camera and paperwork thing got really clear to me.\n\nIt’s not like I woke up one day and everything was different, but looking back, I was doing less. A lot less. Most days when I wasn’t working, I’d just sit in my apartment, and it would be like the whole day went by and I hadn’t really done anything. I’ve got these trees—or plants, whatever—in the corner, and they’d just sit there needing attention, and I’d notice it, but I couldn’t make myself get up and deal with them. Same with food. I’d buy food and it would just stay in the fridge until it went bad, because cooking felt like this whole complicated project even though I used to cook all the time.\n\nAnd I stopped calling the guys to play basketball. That used to be a normal thing for me. I didn’t make some big decision like, “I’m quitting basketball,” I just… didn’t call. Didn’t answer sometimes. Didn’t feel like explaining. It wasn’t even that I was enjoying being alone. I’d just sit there and think I should get up, I should do something, and then I wouldn’t.\n\nSo, yeah, maybe three to six months ago people probably could’ve noticed I wasn’t myself. I didn’t really want to call it anything at the time. I just kept thinking I was tired, or burned out, or I’d catch up later. But I wasn’t doing the regular stuff I used to do, and I couldn’t really get myself to care enough to start.\n\nDoctor: During those three to six months when you were staying home and not doing your usual activities, did you also feel persistently sad, down, or hopeless most of the day, or was it more that you just lacked motivation and energy without feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it “sad” exactly. Not most of the day, every day, like crying or thinking everything was hopeless. It wasn’t that kind of feeling, at least not how I understand it.\n\nIt was more like… I just couldn’t get myself to start anything. Like I’d be sitting in my apartment and the whole day would kind of disappear. The trees in the corner would need water or trimming or whatever, and I’d see them there, I knew they needed something, but I’d just keep sitting. Same with food in the fridge—I’d buy stuff thinking I’d cook like I used to, and then it would just sit there until I had to throw it out. Cooking felt like too much, even though it’s not like I don’t know how to do it.\n\nAnd basketball, too. I used to call the guys, make plans, show up. Then I just stopped. Not because I was sitting there feeling miserable and saying, “What’s the point of life?” It was more like the idea of calling, getting dressed, going out, talking to people—there was no push in me to do it. I couldn’t make myself care enough to start.\n\nSo, yeah, more motivation and energy, I guess. I wasn’t myself, but I don’t think it was exactly being depressed."

Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI guess… they sent me here because of what happened at the station. I’m an EMT, and I was in the bay, and the airway bags were wrong. Not wrong like someone stocked them wrong, but the way they were sitting there, the order of them, it was off, and I knew it had to be put back into the right pattern.\n\nSo I kept moving them around, lining them up the way it needed to be, and I was saying the dispatch numbers out loud while I did it. I know that probably sounds odd, but it felt like if I stopped in the middle, or if I missed one, the whole bay would go bad somehow. My lieutenant kept telling me to step away and stop touching the bags, but I couldn’t just break the sequence like that. That’s why they told me I had to come in.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. I mean, standing here now, talking about it, I can hear how it sounds—me moving the airway bags around and saying the dispatch numbers out loud, over and over, and not stopping when my lieutenant told me to step away. I get that from the outside it probably looked like I was just… stuck on it.\n\nBut in the moment it didn’t feel optional. It felt like the bags had to be in the right pattern, like there was a correct way they were supposed to sit, and if I stopped before it was finished or left one out of place, then the whole bay would go bad somehow. Not like I had a neat explanation for it, but it felt very clear to me right then. Like breaking the sequence would mess everything up.\n\nSo part of me knew my lieutenant was getting upset and that I was supposed to listen, but another part of me was just focused on getting it right. Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick). Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.   }

Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame). Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t think so—not in the way you mean, I don’t think.\n\nLike, I’m not sitting there hearing a voice talking to me when nobody’s around, or seeing a person standing there that other people say isn’t there. I mean, at the station there are always noises—radios, doors, equipment, people moving around—so sometimes I’ll hear something and turn my head, but that’s normal in that place. It’s noisy. It’s not like I can say, “I heard a voice no one else could hear.”\n\nAnd seeing things… no, I don’t think I’ve seen things that weren’t there. I’m careful saying it because I know how this sounds once people start asking these questions, and I don’t want everything I say to get put in the wrong box. My issue isn’t that I’m seeing ghosts or hearing voices.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }

Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick). Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYeah… I guess if I’m being honest, there were changes before the camera and paperwork thing got really clear to me.\n\nIt’s not like I woke up one day and everything was different, but looking back, I was doing less. I’ve got these trees—or plants, whatever—in the corner, and they’d just sit there needing attention, and I’d notice it, but I couldn’t make myself get up and deal with them. Same with food. I’d buy food and it would just stay in the fridge until it went bad, because cooking felt like this whole complicated project even though I used to cook all the time.\n\nAnd I stopped calling the guys to play basketball. That used to be a normal thing for me. I didn’t make some big decision like, “I’m quitting basketball,” I just… didn’t call. Didn’t answer sometimes. Didn’t feel like explaining. It wasn’t even that I was enjoying being alone. I’d just sit there and think I should get up, I should do something, and then I wouldn’t.\n\nSo, yeah, maybe three to six months ago people probably could’ve noticed I wasn’t myself. I didn’t really want to call it anything at the time. I just kept thinking I was tired, or burned out, or I’d catch up later.
